I mean, I can still remember liking those things, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I don’t really feel much pleasure from them now.\n\nI used to jog to clear my head, or knit, or cook, or read after class… and now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and can’t really make myself care about doing any of it. Is that… is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not really like before. I used to feel better after jogging, or I’d get into knitting or cooking or reading after class. Now even if I have time, I mostly just end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs and… I don’t know, I can’t make myself care enough to start. I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vague.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the pleasure you derive from activities that you used to enjoy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… over the past year, yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but things I used to do to feel better—like jogging, or knitting, cooking, reading after class—just don’t really feel rewarding now.\n\nMost days I end up sitting on my bed still in my scrubs, and I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start any of it. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, but… that’s probably the main change I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel better, or has your low mood been constant over the past year?\n\nPatient: I have moments where I can kind of act okay, I guess… like at the clinic, if I’m smiling at patients or speaking Spanish with them, I can seem normal enough. But inside it’s still there most days.\n\nI usually wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m trying to keep it together, it feels like I’m just barely managing. I keep thinking I’m a disappointment, and that I’m not safe to be a nurse.\n\nThere was this near-miss, and I know maybe people make mistakes, but I can’t stop feeling like it proves something about me… like I’m a danger, or I shouldn’t be doing this. And I hate saying this, but part of me feels like everyone would be better off without me around. Is that… is that the kind of thing I’m supposed to tell you?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: I think… about a year, maybe a little more. Like a year and two months, I guess. It started after I graduated high school and began taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. It wasn’t all at once, but that’s when I started feeling this way about myself more and more. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about the same, less, or more than before you started feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I mean, I still tell myself I could do those things, so it’s not like I hate them or anything… but I used to go jogging to clear my head, or knit, or cook something, or read after class.\n\nLately I mostly just… sit on my bed still in my scrubs and don’t really make myself do any of it. I can’t seem to care enough, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I mean, I can still remember liking those things, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I don’t really feel much pleasure from them now.\n\nI used to jog to clear my head, or knit, or cook, or read after class… and now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and can’t really make myself care about doing any of it. Is that… is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not really like before. I used to feel better after jogging, or I’d get into knitting or cooking or reading after class. Now even if I have time, I mostly just end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs and… I don’t know, I can’t make myself care enough to start. I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vague.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the pleasure you derive from activities that you used to enjoy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… over the past year, yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but things I used to do to feel better—like jogging, or knitting, cooking, reading after class—just don’t really feel rewarding now.\n\nMost days I end up sitting on my bed still in my scrubs, and I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start any of it. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, but… that’s probably the main change I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel better, or has your low mood been constant over the past year?\n\nPatient: I have moments where I can kind of act okay, I guess… like at the clinic, if I’m smiling at patients or speaking Spanish with them, I can seem normal enough. But inside it’s still there most days.\n\nI usually wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m trying to keep it together, it feels like I’m just barely managing. I don’t know if that counts as constant, but… it’s there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where you felt completely back to your normal self, without this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I have times where I can look normal, like at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily know.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve felt completely like myself. Most days I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m functioning, it feels like I’m just barely holding myself together. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I don’t want you to think I’m… unsafe in that way.\n\nBut I do have thoughts sometimes like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear so I wouldn’t mess anything up again. I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where your heavy, empty feeling is completely gone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think completely gone, no. I mean… there are parts of the day where I can sort of push it down and look okay, especially at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish with them.\n\nBut most days I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Even when I’m acting normal, it’s more like I’m barely holding myself together than actually feeling fine… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days where you don't wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I wish I could say there are days like that. Most days I wake up and it’s just there, that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nI can still get through the day, and at the clinic I can smile and talk with patients in Spanish and seem normal, but… it doesn’t feel like it’s gone. It feels more like I’m just trying really hard to hold it together. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any periods of time, even if just a few hours, where you feel your normal self without the heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a few minutes here and there, I can get distracted enough to seem like myself… like when I’m with patients and I’m smiling or speaking Spanish with them at the clinic.\n\nBut I don’t think I really feel normal for a few hours. It’s more like the heavy feeling is still there in my chest, and I’m just trying really hard to cover it and keep functioning. I’m sorry, I know I keep describing it the same way, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But inside it’s still there most days.\n\nI usually wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m trying to keep it together, it feels like I’m just barely managing. I don’t know if that counts as constant, but… it’s there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where you felt completely back to your normal self, without this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I have times where I can look normal, like at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily know.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve felt completely like myself. Even when I’m acting normal, it’s more like I’m barely holding myself together than actually feeling fine… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days where you don't wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I wish I could say there are days like that. Most days I wake up and it’s just there, that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nI can still get through the day, and at the clinic I can smile and talk with patients in Spanish and seem normal, but… it doesn’t feel like it’s gone. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or spending time with friends and family, about the same, less, or more than before you started feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: Less, I think. I mean, I still tell myself I could do those things, so it’s not like I hate them or anything… but I used to go jogging to clear my head, or knit, or cook something, or read after class.\n\nLately I mostly just… sit on my bed still in my scrubs and don’t really make myself do any of it. I can’t seem to care enough, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do, or has that changed for you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s changed. I mean, I can still remember liking those things, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I don’t really feel much pleasure from them now.\n\nI used to jog to clear my head, or knit, or cook, or read after class… and now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and can’t really make myself care about doing any of it. Is that… is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or enjoy doing?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but not really like before. I used to feel better after jogging, or I’d get into knitting or cooking or reading after class. Now even if I have time, I mostly just end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs and… I don’t know, I can’t make myself care enough to start. I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds kind of vague.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in the pleasure you derive from activities that you used to enjoy over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… over the past year, yes, I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but things I used to do to feel better—like jogging, or knitting, cooking, reading after class—just don’t really feel rewarding now.\n\nMost days I end up sitting on my bed still in my scrubs, and I can’t seem to make myself care enough to start any of it. I’m sorry if I’m repeating myself, but… that’s probably the main change I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where you feel better, or has your low mood been constant over the past year?\n\nPatient: I have moments where I can kind of act okay, I guess… like at the clinic, if I’m smiling at patients or speaking Spanish with them, I can seem normal enough. But inside it’s still there most days.\n\nI usually wake up with this heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m trying to keep it together, it feels like I’m just barely managing. I don’t know if that counts as constant, but… it’s there a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where you felt completely back to your normal self, without this heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I have times where I can look normal, like at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily know.\n\nBut I don’t think I’ve felt completely like myself. Most days I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and even when I’m functioning, it feels like I’m just barely holding myself together. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t have a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. I don’t want you to think I’m… unsafe in that way.\n\nBut I do have thoughts sometimes like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could just disappear so I wouldn’t mess anything up again. I’m sorry, I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you have days where your heavy, empty feeling is completely gone?\n\nPatient: I don’t think completely gone, no. I mean… there are parts of the day where I can sort of push it down and look okay, especially at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish with them.\n\nBut most days I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest. Even when I’m acting normal, it’s more like I’m barely holding myself together than actually feeling fine… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days where you don't wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in your chest?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I wish I could say there are days like that. Most days I wake up and it’s just there, that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nI can still get through the day, and at the clinic I can smile and talk with patients in Spanish and seem normal, but… it doesn’t feel like it’s gone. It feels more like I’m just trying really hard to hold it together. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any periods of time, even if just a few hours, where you feel your normal self without the heavy, empty feeling?\n\nPatient: Maybe for a few minutes here and there, I can get distracted enough to seem like myself… like when I’m with patients and I’m smiling or speaking Spanish with them at the clinic.\n\nBut I don’t think I really feel normal for a few hours. It’s more like the heavy feeling is still there in my chest, and I’m just trying really hard to cover it and keep functioning. I’m sorry, I know I keep describing it the same way, but that’s honestly what it feels like.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been getting worse over time?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I don’t know if I’d say it’s steadily getting worse… it’s more that it feels pretty constant, if that makes sense.\n\nSome days feel a little harder than others, but I haven’t really been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s not a very clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in how you've been feeling over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Over the past two weeks… I think it’s been mostly the same, honestly. Most days I still wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest.\n\nI can act okay at the clinic, like smiling with patients or speaking Spanish with them, and I don’t think anyone would necessarily notice. But inside it feels like I’m kind of barely holding myself together. Is that the kind of change you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced this heavy, empty feeling for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been a little over a year, I think—about a year and two months.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school, when I began taking college classes and working full-time at the clinic. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but that’s around when I noticed it really becoming a constant thing.\n\nDoctor: Has the heavy, empty feeling you've been experiencing lasted for most of the day, nearly every day, for the past year and two months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can still get through the day and do what I’m supposed to do, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut most days I wake up with that heavy, empty feeling in my chest, and it kind of stays there. Even when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish at the clinic and trying to seem normal, inside I feel like I’m just barely holding myself together. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any periods over the past year where your symptoms felt significantly worse than they do now?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure… I don’t think there were clear periods where it was a lot worse than now. It’s been more like a steady heaviness, if that makes sense.\n\nMaybe some days are harder than others, but I don’t know if I’d call them significantly worse. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, there are moments where I’m distracted or I’m busy and I seem more okay, but I don’t think there’s been a real stretch where I felt significantly better.\n\nIt’s kind of been going on since after I graduated high school and started college classes and working full-time at the clinic—so, about a year and two months now. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced two years or more of feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been about a year and two months, I think.\n\nIt started after I graduated high school and then began college classes while working full-time at the clinic. So it’s been a while, but not two years or more. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way about yourself for most of the day, nearly every day, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Is that… the kind of feeling you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
